## MAX20357A

## General Description

The MAX20357A is a power line communication (PLC) slave  with  ModelGauge ™ m5  EZ  fuel  gauge  and charger. With the MAX20355A, the MAX20357A provides a complete system solution for charging and data  transfer  between  a  charging  case  and  a  batterypowered device over a single contact.

The PLC interface is capable of 100kpbs throughput while simultaneously providing 200mA  of charging/system current  per  channel.  A  4Mbaud  half-duplex  data-only UART  mode  provides  an  easy  and  fast  method  for firmware updates, debugging interface, and factory modes.

The charger on slave device MAX20357A controls the output of the 3.3W buck-boost converter in the MAX20355A with dynamic voltage scaling (DVS).

The  MAX20357A  integrates  an  ultra-low  power  fuel gauge which implements the ModelGauge m5 algorithm. The IC monitors a single-cell battery pack and supports internal current sensing.

Additional features include comprehensive master device  insertion  and  removal  notifications,  moisture detection, overcurrent protection, and 8kV contact-rated ESD protection on the PLC output.

## Applications

- True Wireless Stereo Headphones
- Augmented Reality Glasses
- Wearable Devices

## Simplified Application Circuit

<!-- image -->

ModelGauge is a trademark of Maxim Integrated Products, Inc.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## Benefits and Features

- Power Line Communication (PLC) Interface
- 100kpbs System Throughput
- 167.7kpbs Bit Rate
- 200mA Dual-Slave and 400mA Mono-Slave Output Charging Current
- Automatic Earbud Insertion/Removal Detection
- 4Mbaud, Half-Duplex, Data-Only UART Mode
- PLC Controllable GPIOs, Reset and Shipping Mode
- High-Efficiency Autonomous Charging System
- 90% End-to-End Charging Efficiency from Master to Slave Battery
- 400mA Integrated Linear Charger
- Optimized Charging Efficiency by Automatic DVS Negotiation between Master and Slave
- Robust PLC Output Protection Features
- Highly Flexible Moisture Detection Block
- Input Current Limiting
- 8kV Contact ESD Protection
- Small Solution Size
- Low External Component Count
- 2.69mm x 2.69mm, 36-Bump, 0.4mm Pitch Wafer-Level Package (WLP)

Ordering information appears at end of data sheet.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## Functional Diagram

<!-- image -->

## Detailed Description

The MAX20355A, together with the MAX20357A, form an integrated power line communication (PLC) and automated charging system. These devices provide a complete system solution for efficient charging and data transfer between a charging case and wireless earbuds over a single contact.

The  MAX20355A  and  MAX20357A  feature  data  transfers  while  simultaneously  delivering  200mA  of  charge/system current per output. Both devices support a 166.7kbps bit rate while charging. With a 166.7kbps bit rate, the PLC protocol can achieve up to 130kpbs of throughput using the bulk data transfer feature. Additionally, a 4Mbaud half-duplex dataonly UART mode provides an easy and fast method for firmware updates, debug interface, and factory modes. Finally, dedicated hardware PLC commands allow both devices to control GPIOs and enter various power modes, such as ultralow current ship mode, through the PLC link.

The MAX20355A integrates a completely autonomous charging system that utilizes a 3.3W buck boost with dynamic voltage  scaling  (DVS)  to  provide  an  optimized  charge  voltage  to  the  earbuds.  An  integrated  digital  state  machine automatically manages the dynamic voltage adjustment based on the earbud battery voltage to minimize earbud power dissipation and maximize charging efficiency.

Additional features include comprehensive earbud insertion and removal notifications, even in dead case or bud battery scenarios, and moisture detection on the PLC outputs to prevent contact corrosion. The PLC pins are protected from overcurrent events by an integrated current limiting circuit and ESD events by integrated 8kV contact-rated ESD protection structures.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## Power Line Communication (PLC)

The  MAX20355A/MAX20357A  PLC  interface  offers  a  means  by  which  charging  and  exchange  of  data  can  occur simultaneously on a single wire connection. The interface can accommodate 100kbps of throughput and can do so while delivering up to 200mA of current per channel in dual slave mode and up to 400mA in mono slave mode. The MAX20355A has an integrated current sink and resistor R TX  as shown in Figure 1 . During communication, the MAX20355A utilizes the pulses generated by the PLC current sink over resistor R TX  to create PLC pulses on the PLC line. The MAX20355A uses PLC pulses to transfer information to the MAX20357A. At the same time, the charging function is not affected. The MAX20357A uses a similar approach to create PLC pulses and transfer information over the PLC line. When there is no ongoing PLC communication, R TX  is shorted by a bypass switch to achieve higher overall power transfer efficiency.

Figure 1. Simplified Scheme of Power Line Communication (PLC)

<!-- image -->

The PLC interface balances between robustness and throughput. Synchronization mechanisms and checksums offer robust data transfer, but this is also balanced against protocol overhead. With a 166.67kbps bit rate, this careful balance allows a low error rate while achieving throughput up to 130kbps in bulk data transfer mode. To overcome limitations on throughput due to the I 2 C interface, the MAX20355A/MAX20357A integrates two 128-byte FIFOs. This allows the system to keep I 2 C overhead low utilizing bulk writes. The MAX20355A also features a 4Mbaud, data-only, half-duplex UART passthrough mode. This mode uses a simple switch that connects UART ports from master to slave for firmware updates, factory mode, and debug mode over a single PLC line.

The following sections describe the PLC PING (PLC command/mailbox data transfer), FIFO (bulk data transfer), and UART interface in detail.

## PLC PING

The PLC interface between master and slave follows a master-initiated scheme. There are two data transfer types -PING and bulk data transfer. PINGs begin with a transfer from master to slave to send data across the interface, request data from the slave, offer the slave a chance to make requests, and sometimes issue commands to the slave. Figure 2 shows the basic structure of the master-to-slave PING packet. The master initiates the transmission with a preamble which synchronizes the link and then transmits data. The slave responds in the same manner within a response timeout period. PLC PINGs are automatically generated and transmitted through the PLC line every telemetry period.

Figure 2. Periodic PING

<!-- image -->

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## LISTEN Command and Continuously Updated Information

Each PLC PING contains one PLC command. Most PLC commands are initiated by an I 2 C write from the system. However, the MAX20355A features an automatic LISTEN command, which is sent periodically independent of input from the I 2 C interface. When there is no customer PLC command issued through I 2 C, the LISTEN command is automatically sent through the PING, the slave has the option to send its command back to the master using its command field. In response to the master's PING, the slave sends the appropriate da ta based on the contents of the master PING. The contents of the command and data fields are filled according to the requirements of the command sent from the master. The periodic PING offers the following features:

1. Send master PLC command from master to slave ( Table 1 ).
2. Give slave an opportunity to send slave PLC commands to master ( Table 2 ).
3. Transmit GPIO status between master and slave.
4. Exchange master/slave battery information: master battery voltage, slave battery voltage, slave SoC, and slave charging status.
5. Automatic PLC voltage adjustment to track slave battery voltage and minimize power consumption. Details are described in the Charger Battery Voltage Tracking Loop section.
6. Automatic disconnection detection. Master detects slave is disconnected if there is no proper slave response for master PING.

The exchanged GPIO status and battery information between master and slave are ready to read from the master and slave local registers.

## Master Commands

In addition to the LISTEN command, which is handled automatically, the master can execute other commands manually as well. The master's command field determines what type of transaction occurs on the PLC interface. Commands over the PLC interface are requested by I 2 C. Table 1 describes the various commands the master can send and what data and statuses are exchanged. Note that many commands can be sent from master to slave or from slave to master. However, it is only in response to a LISTEN command where the slave may send its commands. Master commands are initiated by writing to the plc\_command1/2 and triggered by plc\_run\_trg1/2 through master I 2 C. In the command argument register plc\_cmd\_arg1/2, details on how the command is processed are available for the user to specify.

## Table 1. Master Commands

| MASTER COMMANDS   | plc_command1/2   | plc_cmd_arg1/2                       | plc_cmd_arg1/2                                                                   |
|-------------------|------------------|--------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| LISTEN            | N/A              | N/A                                  | N/A                                                                              |
| SET_GPIO          | 0x3              | New slave GPIO setting               | New slave GPIO setting                                                           |
| DOUT_REQ          | 0x5              | Number of bytes (up to 128 bytes)    | Number of bytes (up to 128 bytes)                                                |
| UART_REQ          | 0x6              | slave_uart_sw[1:0]                   | slave_uart_sw[1:0]                                                               |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x00                                 | SEAL request. Puts the slave in SEAL mode.                                       |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x01                                 | Soft reset request. Resets slave's registers.                                    |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x02                                 | Hard reset request. Cycle the power on the slave's SYS node.                     |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x03                                 | Fuel gauge reset request. Resets fuel gauge block.                               |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x04                                 | FIFO request. Puts system into bulk data transfer mode.                          |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x05                                 | Free request. Stop bulk data transfer and free the data line.                    |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x06                                 | IDLE mode request. Sends system into PLC IDLE mode.                              |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | 0x07                                 | Hard + Soft reset request. Reset all registers, FSM, and cycle the power at SYS. |
| SYST_REQ          | 0x0              | {0x3F, slave_uart_tx, slave_uart_rx} | UBOOT request. Hard resets slave and puts slave in UART mode.                    |

## Slave Commands

The slave can also issue commands to the master. The slave similarly issues the commands based on I 2 C input, but it must wait for  a  master  LISTEN  command before the  command can be sent back  to the master.  Once the LISTEN command is received, the slave then sends its command using the COMMAND field. Figure 3 shows the method by which the slave can send a command. Slave commands are initiated with a write to plc\_command and triggered by plc\_run\_trg through master I 2 C. In the command argument register plc\_cmd\_arg, details on how to process the PLC commands are available for the user to specify.

Figure 3. Sending Commands from the Slave

<!-- image -->

## Table 2. Slave Commands

| SLAVE COMMAND*   | plc_command   | plc_cmd_arg                       | plc_cmd_arg                                                       |
|------------------|---------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| SET_GPIO         | 0x3           | New master GPIO setting           | New master GPIO setting                                           |
| DOUT_REQ         | 0x5           | Number of bytes (up to 128 bytes) | Number of bytes (up to 128 bytes)                                 |
| UART_REQ         | 0x6           | master_uart_sw[3:0]               | master_uart_sw[3:0]                                               |
| SYST_REQ         | 0x0           | 0x01                              | Soft reset request. Resets master's registers.                    |
| SYST_REQ         | 0x0           | 0x03                              | Fuel gauge reset request. Resets master's fuel gauge block.       |
| SYST_REQ         | 0x0           | 0x04                              | FIFO request. Puts master and slave into bulk data transfer mode. |
| SYST_REQ         | 0x0           | 0x05                              | Free request. Stop bulk data transfer and free the data line.     |
| SYST_REQ         | 0x0           | 0x06                              | Idle mode request. Sends master into idle mode.                   |

## Mailbox Data Transfer (DOUT\_REQ)

## From Master to Slave

Mailbox data transfers are used to exchange one data packet, up to 128 bytes, from master to slave. A mailbox data transfer begins with a data out request (DOUT\_REQ) master command. Fill the data packet into the master RAM, write the number of bytes to be transferred in the command argument field plc\_cmd\_arg1/2, then trigger the DOUT\_REQ command. If the slave responds ACK to the DOUT\_REQ command, data in RAM is transferred to the slave automatically. After the slave successfully receives data, plc\_new\_dat interrupt is asserted and RAM\_is\_full is set to 1. Once RAM\_is\_full is set to 1, slave RAM is protected from PLC and it sends NACK to the master's DOUT\_REQ command until RAM\_is\_full is write cleared. Make sure to read data in RAM before clearing RAM\_is\_full to avoid data loss. Note that although both PLC devices have two integrated 128-byte RAMs, only one RAM is used in mailbox data transfer. Figure 4 shows the flow of data during a master to slave write.

Figure 4. Master to Slave Mailbox Data Transfer

<!-- image -->

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## From Slave to Master

A similar scheme is used to send a packet of data, up to 128 bytes, from slave to master. A mailbox data transfer begins with a data out request (DOUT\_REQ) slave command. Write the number of bytes to be transferred in the command argument field plc\_cmd\_arg. Fill the data packet into the slave RAM, write the number of bytes to be transferred in the command argument field plc\_cmd\_arg, then trigger the DOUT\_REQ command. Since the system follows the masterinitiated scheme, the slave triggers the DOUT\_REQ command when it receives the master Listen command. After the master receives DOUT\_REQ and responds to ACK, data in RAM is transferred to the slave automatically. After the master successfully receives data, plc\_new\_dat interrupt is asserted and RAM\_is\_full is set to 1. Once RAM\_is\_full is set to 1, master RAM is protected from PLC and it sends NACK to the slave's DOUT\_REQ command until RAM\_is\_full is write cleared. Make sure to read data in RAM before clearing RAM\_is\_full to avoid data loss. Note that although both PLC devices have two integrated 128-byte RAMs, only one RAM is used in mailbox data transfer. Figure 5 shows the flow of data during a slave to master write.

Figure 5. Slave to Master Mailbox Data Transfer

<!-- image -->

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## Bulk Data Transfer (FIFO)

To minimize the impact of I 2 C protocol overhead on the achievable throughput, MAX20357A and MAX20357A integrate two 128-byte RAMs for bulk data transfer. The bulk data transfer scheme is shown in Figure 6 . The I 2 C bus connects first to the RAM\_1 to load data with I 2 C bulk writes at data rates up to 400kbps. Once the DOUT\_REQ command is triggered, the I 2 C bus and PLC line are swapped, I 2 C bus from RAM\_1 to RAM\_2 and PLC from RAM\_2 to RAM\_1. While RAM\_1 is being emptied by the PLC engine, the next set of data can be loaded by I 2 C bulk write again into the RAM\_2. An interrupt is sent to the system after RAM\_1 is completely emptied. Repeat the same process to swap the I 2 C bus and PLC back and forth to load and transmit data simultaneously. A similar implementation on the receiving side swaps the I 2 C bus and PLC back and forth to receive and read data to the receiver's microcontroller. Dual -RAM architecture reduces I 2 C protocol overhead and enhances achievable throughput. Packets of any size up to 128 bytes can be sent by this method.

Figure 6. Bulk Data Transfer Implementation

<!-- image -->

## MAX20357A

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## From Master to Slave

Bulk data transfers from master to slave begin with a system request (SYST\_REQ) master command. In the command argument of SYST\_REQ, the master needs to send a FIFO request. Once receiving an ACK from the slave, the PLC voltage is pulled up to the communication voltage, and the PLC line is locked for bulk data transfer. Before triggering the data transfer, the data packet needs to be filled into the master RAM through I 2 C. Similar to mailbox data transfer, write the  number  of  bytes  to  be  transferred  to  plc\_cmd\_trg1/2,  then  trigger  the  DOUT\_REQ  command  and  wait  for plc1/2\_cmd\_dne interrupt. Repeat DOUT\_REQ and wait for plc1/2\_cmd\_dne to interrupt multiple times as needed. After all data packets are transferred, issue a FREE request from the master side to unlock the line and return to normal PLC mode. Note that master bulk data transfer is designed to transfer data only from master to slave. Figure 7 shows the flow of data during a master to slave write.

Figure 7. Master to Slave Bulk Data Transfer

<!-- image -->

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## From Slave to Master

Bulk data transfers from slave to master begin with a system request (SYST\_REQ) slave command. In the command argument of SYST\_REQ, the slave needs to write a FIFO request. The slave waits until the master's LISTEN command to send out a FIFO request through the PLC line. Once receiving an ACK from the master, the master locks the PLC line and enters bulk data transfer mode. Before triggering the data transfer, the data packet needs to be filled into the slave RAM. Similar to mailbox data transfer,  write  the  number  of  bytes  to  be  transferred  to  plc\_cmd\_arg,  then  trigger  the DOUT\_REQ command and wait for plc\_cmd\_dne interrupt. Repeat DOUT\_REQ and wait for plc\_cmd\_dne to interrupt multiple times as needed. After all data packets are transferred, issue a FREE request from the slave side to unlock the line and return to normal PLC mode. Note that slave bulk data transfer is designed to transfer data only from slave to master. Figure 8 shows the flow of data during a slave to master write.

Figure 8. Slave to Master Bulk Data Transfer

<!-- image -->

## Half-Duplex UART Passthrough Interface

The MAX20355A and MAX20357A feature a 4Mbaud, data-only, half-duplex UART passthrough mode. This mode uses switches that connect either the TX or RX between the master and slave SoC for firmware updates, factory mode, and debug mode over the single PLC line. A UART command can be sent through the PLC interface to put the PLC devices into  UART mode in a particular configuration. The configuration can be seen in Figure 9 .  The  user can use the I 2 C command to switch roles between TX to RX on master and slave. The PLC solution provides automatic UART switch control by detecting incoming data, which does not require I 2 C access in UART mode.

Figure 9. UART Interface

<!-- image -->

## From Master to Slave (I 2 C and Auto Switch Control)

The master can put the slave into UART mode by using the UART\_REQ master command. The direction of UART communication  is  set  in  the  PLC  command  argument.  Once  the  UART  command  is  successfully  triggered,  the corresponding master and slave switch after 25ms  of delay. The 25ms delay is used to make sure the PLC line is disconnected from the master's buck -boost converter and UART switches on both sides can be closed safely. The switch settings are shown in Table 3 . All the master/slave UART switch settings can be changed through the local I 2 C command after entering UART mode.

The PLC solution features I 2 C-free automatic UART switch control. By detecting incoming data, the PLC chipsets can automatically close the corresponding UART switches. This feature can be used only when a UART request is issued from master to slave with slave\_uart\_sw = 0b10. Enable UART auto mode by setting urt\_auto\_en2/1 = 1 (MAX20355A) and urt\_auto\_en = 1 (MAX20357A) before the master UART\_REQ command is issued. Once the PLC devices enter UART mode, the default UART switch position is shown in Table 3 . Then the master starts to detect the falling edge on the UART\_T pin and the slave starts to detect the falling edge on the UART\_RX pin. If one signal falling edge is captured on UART\_T or UART\_RX, the PLC device toggles the switch from UART\_R (master)/UART\_TX (slave) to UART\_T (master)/UART\_RX (slave) automatically. Note that in the UART auto mode, PLC devices assume there is no incoming data from both the master and slave microcontroller simultaneously. The UART switch toggles once a falling edge is detected ignoring the possibility of data conflicts. The system designer needs to prevent sending data on both sides at the same time. After the data transfer is completed, PLC devices wait for a blanking period, then toggle the switch back from UART\_T (master)/UART\_RX (slave) to UART\_R (master)/UART\_TX (slave) automatically. The blanking time is I 2 C programmable and controlled by tx\_tmo\_tim (MAX20355A) and rx\_tmo\_tim (MAX20357A). This feature allows the user to exchange data through UART without I 2 C intervention.

## Table 3. UART Switch Settings in Master PLC Command Argument

| slave_uart_sw[1:0]                      | MASTER UART_T   | MASTER UART_R   | SLAVE UART_TX   | SLAVE UART_RX   |
|-----------------------------------------|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------|
| 0b00 : All switches off                 | ×               | ×               | ×               | ×               |
| 0b01 : RX direction                     | ×               | √               | ×               | √               |
| 0b10 (urt_auto_en2/1 = 0): TX direction | √               | ×               | √               | ×               |
| 0b10 (urt_auto_en2/1 = 1): Auto mode    | ×               | √               | √               | ×               |
| 0b11 : All switches on                  | √               | √               | √               | √               |

MAX20357A

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## From Slave to Master (I 2 C Switch Control)

The slave can put the master into UART mode by using the UART\_REQ slave command. Once the UART command is successfully triggered, corresponding master switches are closed after a 25ms delay. The switch settings are shown in Table  4 .    The  slave  is  not  automatically  sent  to  UART  mode  after  UART\_REQ  and  no  switch  is  closed.  The  slave UART\_TX and UART\_RX switches need to be closed through the local slave I 2 C write. Note that to make sure the buckboost converter is disconnected from the PLC line, the slave checks if the PLC pin can be pulled low before entering UART mode. If master UART\_T/UART\_R is pulled high, the slave has 23ms to issue an I 2 C command to enter UART mode after receiving ACK for the PLC UART\_REQ command. If the slave I 2 C UART command is delayed for more than 23ms, it causes slave UART failure. All the master/slave UART switch settings can be changed through the local I 2 C command after entering UART mode.

## Table 4. UART Switch Settings in Slave PLC Command Argument

| master_uart_sw[3:0]                  | MASTER PLC2 UART_T   | MASTER PLC2 UART_R   | MASTER PLC1 UART_T   | MASTER PLC1 UART_R   |
|--------------------------------------|----------------------|----------------------|----------------------|----------------------|
| 0b0000 : All master switches off     | ×                    | ×                    | ×                    | ×                    |
| 0b0001 : Master PLC1 RX direction    | ×                    | ×                    | ×                    | √                    |
| 0b0010 : Master PLC1 TX direction    | ×                    | ×                    | √                    | ×                    |
| 0b0011 : Master PLC1 all switches on | ×                    | ×                    | √                    | √                    |
| 0b0100 : Master PLC2 RX direction    | ×                    | √                    | ×                    | ×                    |
| 0b1000 : Master PLC2 TX direction    | √                    | ×                    | ×                    | ×                    |
| 0b1100 : Master PLC1 all switches on | √                    | √                    | ×                    | ×                    |

'√' represents the switch is ON and '×' represents the switch is OFF

## Exit UART Mode

The following are two ways to quit the UART mode:

1. Master/slave can quit UART mode by disabling UART mode and disconnecting the UART switch through the master/slave I 2 C.
2. Use master and slave UART timeout counter and wait for UART timeout interrupt.

Enable timeout counter through tmo\_tmr\_ena1/2 on the master and tmo\_tmr\_ena on the slave before entering UART mode. Three situations can affect UART timeout duration:

1. When no pull-up on the UART line for both master and slave:

When not detecting activity on the UART line, the master starts a 0.75s UART timeout counter, this timer will be reset when any activity, more precisely, falling edge, on the UART line is detected. Once the TX/RX line is idle for 0.5s, the slave turns off its UART switch, disconnects UART\_TX or UART\_RX from the PLC line, and starts a counter to count for 0.6s. At 0.512s the master disables its PLC comparator and does not detect any PLC activity. Meanwhile, once the line is idle for 0.75s, the master turns off its UART switch, disconnects UART\_T or UART\_R from the PLC line, connects to the PLC engine, and returns to the PLC detection mode. At 1.1s after the line is idle, the 0.6s slave UART exit timer expires, the slave exits UART mode, connects the PLC line to the PLC engine, and returns to the PLC detection mode. Note that once the slave disconnects its UART switch at time point 0.5s, it enters an automatic UART exit process and this process is irreversible. It is not allowed to send data from the master through UART after time point 0.5s. If data resumes after 0.5s, the master may be held in UART mode. This causes an issue because the slave exits UART mode at time point 1.1s and starts to send the PLC detection pulse immediately. If the master is still in UART mode after the slave exits UART mode, the slave's PLC detection pulse may hold the master in UART mode.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

Figure 10. UART Automatic Exit Through UART Timeout with No Pull-Up

<!-- image -->

## 2. When pull-up on the master side UART line:

When not detecting activity on the UART line, the master starts a 0.75s UART timeout counter, this timer is reset when any activity, more precisely, falling edge, on the UART line is detected. Once the TX/RX line is idle for 0.5s, the slave turns off its UART switch, disconnects UART\_TX or UART\_RX from the PLC line, and starts a counter to count for 0.6s. At 0.512s master disables its PLC comparator and does not detect any PLC activity. This generates a falling edge on the master UART line that resets the 0.75s master UART timeout counter. At 1.1s after the line is idle, the 0.6s slave UART exit timer expires, the slave exits UART mode, connects the PLC line to the PLC engine, and returns to the PLC detection mode. At 1.262s, the master's 0.75s UART timeout counter expires, the master turns off its UART switch, disconnects UART\_T or UART\_R from the PLC line, connects to the PLC engine, and returns to the PLC detection mode. Note that once the slave disconnects its UART switch at time point 0.5s, it enters an automatic UART exit process and this process is irreversible. It is not allowed to send data from the master through UART after time point 0.5s. If data resumes after 0.5s, the master may be held in UART mode. This causes an issue because the slave exits UART mode at time point 1.1s and starts to send the PLC detection pulse immediately. If the master is still in UART mode after the slave exits UART mode, the slave's PLC detection pulse may hold the master in UART mode.

Figure 11. UART Automatic Exit Through UART Timeout with Pull-Up on Master UART Line

<!-- image -->

## 3. When pull-up on the slave side UART line:

When not detecting activity on the UART line, the master starts a 0.75s UART timeout counter, this timer will be reset when any activity, more precisely, falling edge, on the UART line is detected. Once the TX/RX line is idle for 0.5s, the slave turns off its UART switch, disconnects UART\_TX or UART\_RX from the PLC line, and starts a counter to count for 0.6s. This generates a falling edge on the master UART line that resets the 0.75s master UART timeout counter. At 0.512s master disables its PLC comparator and will not detect any PLC activity. At 1.1s after the line is idle, the 0.6s slave UART exit timer expires, the slave exits UART mode, connects the PLC line to the PLC engine, and returns to the PLC detection mode. At 1.25s, the master's 0.75s UART time out counter expires, the master turns off its UART switch, disconnects UART\_T or UART\_R from the PLC line, connects to the PLC engine, and returns to the PLC detection mode. Note that

## UART Overvoltage Protection

If the PLC chipsets do not exit UART mode properly, there is a risk that the master exits UART mode first, starts to send a PLC pulse, and treats slave UART ports as a slave with a dead battery (See the PLC Detection Mode section for details). It is highly recommended that system designers implement firmware protection to make sure the slave exits UART mode earlier than the master device. On MAX20357A, an overvoltage protection feature is offered in UART mode to add a second layer of protection in case firmware malfunctions. If MAX20357A is in UART mode, and the voltage on the PLC pin is above 2.2V, the MAX20357A opens all the UART switches and stays in this state until the MAX20357A is fully reset. This feature can be disabled by setting urt\_ovrv\_en to 0.

## Autonomous Optimized Battery Charging

The MAX20355A interacts with MAX20357A to create an autonomous, closed-loop battery voltage-tracking charging system. This system utilizes a dynamic voltage scaling (DVS) buck boost on the MAX20355A in conjunction with an ultralow dropout charger (ULDO) on the MAX20357A. The PLC interface closes the loop allowing the buck-boost to adjust its output voltage to accommodate the minimum required headroom on the charger. This method allows for excellent 90% efficient energy transfer from the case battery to the earbud battery without the need to place bulky inductors on the tiny form factor earbuds. The excellent efficiency and extremely low heat generation of this charging system offer an increase in the number of charging cycles that can be supported by the case battery as well as the option to increase the rate of charge and deliver faster recharge times to the end customer.

## Charger Battery Voltage Tracking Loop

Figure 13 shows the structure of the automatic charger battery voltage tracking algorithm. The charger battery voltage tracking algorithm is accomplished with data exchanges initiated by the master and responded to by the slave(s). Once the connection is built between master and slave(s), the master connects the buck-boost regulator to the PLC line and holds the PLC voltage to bb\_vlt\_\_def (3.5V by default) for 100ms. Then pull the PLC line to communication voltage (5.5V by default) and start to PING. The communication voltage is programmable through bb\_vlt\_tran. The slave responds with an  ACK  packet  after  the  master  sends  the  first  LISTEN  command  during  PING.  The  slave's  ACK  packet  contains information including slave GPIO status, battery information, charging status, and PLC voltage up/down request. The master adjusts buck-boost output based on the PLC voltage up/down request from slave(s).

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

Figure 13. Polling Structure of Automatic Charging Algorithm

<!-- image -->

The slave compares the battery voltage with the PLC line voltage. If the PLC input voltage is above the programmed upper threshold (PLC voltage is in the decrease zone), the slave sends a 'decrease' signal during PING. Similarly, if PLC voltage is below th e lower limit (increase zone) or within the limit (hold zone), it sends an 'increase' or 'hold' signal accordingly. The voltage thresholds are fully programmable through the parameters shown in Figure 14 . The minimum step of voltage adjustment is programmable through I 2 C register bits up\_down\_val.

Figure 14. Slave Voltage Control Algorithm

<!-- image -->

## Ultra-Low Dropout Battery Charger

The battery charger on MAX20357A is a fully featured lithium-ion charger offering I 2 C programmable voltage thresholds through charging phases: precharge voltage VPChg, step charging voltage ChgStepRise, charge termination voltage BatReg, and top-off current IChgDone. The MAX20357A also features safety timers, a JEITA charging profile with a thermistor, and overtemperature protection with a die temperature sensor. It starts charging from the precharge stage if the battery voltage is lower than the precharge voltage VPChg. The step charging feature allows a higher charge rate at the beginning of the charge cycle during CC1. Once the battery voltage rises to step charge threshold ChgStepRise, the charger transitions to CC2 at a lower charge rate. If the battery voltage is charged up close to BatReg, there are two options to charge in the final stage: CC track mode and CV mode. In CC track mode, the charger current is regulated and reduced by a fixed step as the battery charges up. In CV mode, the BAT pin voltage is regulated at BatReg, and the charging current naturally reduces as the battery charges up. As shown in Figure 15 ,  in  CC track mode, the charger current is evaluated roughly every 1.3ms, if the charge current hits the natural charge current controlled by CV mode (dash line in Figure 15 ), the MAX20357A reduces the charge current by 1/64 of CC2IFChg. The charger controls current directly in CC track mode, which prevents potential abrupt current change that is common in CV mode. The PLC line is protected from fluctuation of charger current which could lead to communication failure. CC track feature is enabled by default. It is highly recommended to keep the default setting to maintain a clean PLC line for reliable PLC communication.

The charging current options for precharge, CC1, and CC2 can be found in IPChg, CC1IFChg, and CC2IFChg. The voltage threshold from precharge to CC1, from CC1 to CC2, and charge termination voltage can be found in VPChg, ChgStepRise, and BatReg. The top-off current threshold can be found in IChgDone. The step charging profile is shown in Figure 15 .

Figure 15. Step Charging Profile

<!-- image -->

There are also independently programmable temperature thresholds, which allow the user to select in which temperature ranges  precharge,  CC1,  CC2,  and  battery  regulation  voltage  may  operate.  A  different  JEITA-compliant  temperature operation strategy is I 2 C programmable. The timeout function for precharge, CC1, and full charge cycle is also designed to prevent overcharge of the battery. Detailed charger operation is shown in Figure 16 .

## Charger-Off State

As shown in Figure 16 ,  when a PLC connection is not established, power is not applied from the MAX20355A. The MAX20357A charger is in the Charger Off state (ChgStat = 0b0000). After MAX20357A detects the PLC connection, buck-boost voltage is applied to the PLC pin, chn\_con\_sts status is set to 1 and chn\_con\_int interrupt is asserted at the first telemetry PING, signaling PLC connection is established. The charger transitions from the Charger Off state to the Charger IDLE state (ChgStat = 0b0001).

## Charger IDLE State

As shown in Figure 16 , the following events cause the charger to enter the Charger IDLE state.

- PLC connection event. The charger transitions from the Charger Off state  to  the Charger IDLE state  when  the MAX20357A detects a PLC connection event.
- Disable the charger when power is applied to the PLC pin. If power is applied to the PLC pin, the charger enters the Charger IDLE state when the charger is disabled ChgEn = 0.

The charger exits the Charger IDLE state and starts the boot sequence when all the following conditions are met:

- The system is not in PLC IDLE mode. The system could be put into PLC IDLE mode to reduce power dissipation after the earbud battery is fully charged. In PLC IDLE mode, if the charger is enabled ChgEn = 1 and the charger autorestart is enabled (ChgAutoReSta = 1), the system resumes from PLC IDLE mode automatically when the battery voltage drops below recharge threshold BatReg -BatReChg.
- The charger is enabled ChgEn = 1.

When the charger enters the Charger Boot state, all charger timers reset. If chg\_res\_ena = 1, the charger resets all charger-related registers when it goes through the boot sequence.

## Precharge State

As shown in Figure 16 , the charger enters the precharge state (ChgStat = 0b0010) after the charger boot sequence is completed and the SYS pin voltage is regulated higher than or equal to the minimum system voltage SysMin. If the battery voltage is lower than VPChg or FrcPChg = 1, the charger remains in a precharge state. In the precharge state, the battery charging current is IPChg.

The following events cause the charger to exit the precharge state:

- Battery voltage rises above VPChg. If step-charging is enabled CC1Enable = 1, CC1 is not configured to run at room temperature only CC1RoomOnly = 0, or CC1 is configured to run at room temperature only CC1RoomOnly = 1, and the  thermistor  monitoring  reports  room  temperature,  the  charger  enters Fast-Charge Constant Current Mode 1 (CC1) state. By default, step-charging is enabled CC1Enable = 1 and CC1 is configured to run at any temperature CC1RoomOnly = 0.
- Battery  voltage  rises  above  VPChg.  If  step-charging  is  disabled  CC1Enable  =  0,  or  CC1Enable  =  1  and  CC1  is configured to run at room temperature only CC1RoomOnly = 1 but the battery is in a cool or warm temperature zone, the charger enters Fast-Charge Constant Current Mode 2 (CC2) state.
- Precharge timeout. If the charger remains in this state for longer than the charger precharge timer PChgTmr, the charger  enters Charger  Fault-Precharge  Timer  PChgTmr  Expired state  (ChgStat  =  0b1000)  and  suspends charging.
- Charger safety timeout. If the charger safety timer ChgTmr expires while in the precharge state, the charger enters the Charger Fault-Safety Timer ChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1001) and suspends charging.
- JEITA disable charging. The charger suspends charging due to temperature. See the JEITA compliance section for a detailed description.
- Forced precharge. The charger can be forced to operate with precharge current IPChg in Force precharge state by setting FrcPChg = 1. The charger transitions to the charger boot sequence when disabling forced precharge FrcPChg = 0.

## Fast-Charge Constant Current CC1 State

Once the battery voltage rises above the precharge threshold VPChg, the charger allows a higher charge rate CC1IFChg at  the  beginning  of  the  charge  cycle  during Fast-Charge  Constant  Current  Mode  1 (CC1)  state.  Set  register  bit CC1Enable = 0 to disable the step-charging feature and the charger transits from the precharge state directly to the CC2 state.  In  the  JEITA-compliant  thermistor  temperature  monitoring  control,  CC1  can  be  configured  to  run  at  room temperature only by setting CC1RoomOnly = 1. In the CC1 state,  charge  current  is  regulated  less  than  or  equal  to CC1FChg. See the Reduced Charge Current section for reasons that the charge current may be less than CC1IFChg.

MAX20357A

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

The following events cause the charger state machine to exit the CC1 state:

- Battery voltage rises to ChgStepRise. The charger enters the CC2 state.
- Battery voltage drops below VPChg. The charger goes through Fast-Charge CC1 Done , CC2 state, and eventually goes back to the precharge state. During this process, the charger marks the internal signal StepChgDone = 1. If the battery voltage rises above VPChg again, it skips the CC1 state to protect the battery.
- CC1 timeout. If the step-charge timer is enabled CC1TmoLimit = 1 and the charger remains in this state for longer than CC1FChgTmr, it enters the CC2 state.
- Charger safety timeout. If the charger safety timer ChgTmr expires while in the CC1 state, the charger enters the Charger Fault-Safety Timer ChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1001) and suspends charging.
- Temperature measured from THM is out of room temperature zone and CC1 is allowed to operate at room temperature only. If CC1RoomOnly = 1 and the battery temperature transitions from Room to Cool or Warm, the charger enters the CC2 state.
- JEITA disable charging. The charger suspends charging due to temperature. See the JEITA compliance section for a detailed description.

The  battery  charger  dissipates  the  most  power  in  the  fast-charge  constant  current  mode,  which  causes  the  die temperature to rise. If the die temperature approaches ChgThrmLim = 115°C, see the Thermal Regulation section for charger operation details.

## Fast-Charge Constant Current (CC2) State

The step-charge feature is enabled by default on the MAX20357A. Once the battery voltage rises above the step-charge threshold ChgStepRise, the charger enters the Fast-Charge Constant Current Mode 2 (CC2) state with a lower charge rate CC2IFChg to avoid lithium plating and prolong the lifetime of the battery. ChgStepHys field sets the hysteresis for the step charge function to avoid hopping between the CC1 state and CC2 state caused by high voltage drop from current reduction. In the CC2 state, charge current is regulated less than or equal to CC2IFChg. See the Reduced Charge Current section for reasons that the charge current may be less than CC2IFChg.

The following events cause the charger state machine to exit the CC2 state:

- Battery voltage rises to BatReg. CC Track is enabled by default chg\_cc\_trk = 1, the charger enters the CC Track state. If CC Track is disabled chg\_cc\_trk = 0, the charger enters the Fast-Charge Constant Voltage (CV) state. It is highly recommended to keep CC Track enabled for better performance.
- Battery voltage drops below ChgStepRise -ChgStepHys. The charger goes back to the CC1 state.
- Battery voltage drops below VPChg. The charger goes back to the Precharge state.
- Charger safety timeout. If the charger safety timer ChgTmr expires while in CC2 state, the charger enters the Charger Fault-Safety Timer ChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1001) and suspends charging.
- JEITA disable charging. The Charger suspends charging due to temperature. See the JEITA compliance section for a detailed description.

Pogo pin resistance may impose a limit on the maximum allowed step-charge threshold and CC2 charging current at termination voltage BatReg to prevent PLC communication failure.

## Constant Current Tracking State CC Track

The charger enters Constant Current Tracking (CC Track) state when the battery voltage is close to the termination voltage BatReg if CC track is enabled chg\_cc\_trk = 1. In the CC Track state, charger current is evaluated roughly every 1.3ms. If the charge current hits the CV mode charger current at the same battery voltage, the charger reduces the charge current by one fixed step 1/64 of CC2IFChg as shown in Figure 15 . Charge current is reduced with controlled steps until charge current is less than termination current threshold IChgDone.

The following events cause the charger state machine to exit CC Track mode:

- Maximum charger current reduction is reached (charger current is reduced to 0.2 x CC2IFChg) or charger current is reduced below IChgDone, and charger is not in any current reduction mode. The charger enters the Maintain Charge state and starts the Maintain Charge timer.
- Charger safety timeout. If the charger safety timer ChgTmr expires while in CC Track state, the charger enters the Charger Fault-Safety Timer ChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1001) and suspends charging.
- JEITA disable charging. The Charger suspends charging due to temperature. See the JEITA compliance section for a detailed description.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

- CC Track state maintains a clean PLC line for data transfer and keeps battery voltage below the regulation point in the meantime. By default, the CC track is enabled. It is highly recommended to use this feature for more reliable PLC communication when the earbud battery voltage is near fully charged.

## Fast-Charge Constant Voltage (CV) State

As shown in Figure 16 , if the CC track is disabled, the charger enters the Fast-Charge Constant Voltage (CV) state when the battery voltage is close to the top-off voltage BatReg. In the CV state, the charger regulates the battery voltage at BatReg and the charge current decreases from CC2IFChg as the battery voltage increases. Battery regulation voltage can be less than BatReg due to temperature. See the JEITA Compliance section for JEITA reduction of battery regulation voltage.

The following events cause the charger state machine to exit the CV state:

- The charge current drops below IChgDone and the charger is not in any current reduction mode, it enters the Maintain Charge state and starts the Maintain Charge timer MtChgTmr.
- Charger safety timeout. If the charger safety timer ChgTmr expires while in the CV state, the charger enters the Charger Fault-Safety Timer ChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1001) and suspends charging.
- JEITA disable charging. The charger suspends charging due to temperature. See the JEITA compliance section for a detailed description.
- Battery voltage drops. Reduced battery voltage does not directly lead to CV state exit. As the battery voltage drops, the charge current keeps increasing. The charger enters the CC2 state when the charge current hits CC2IFChg.

In CV mode, there might be a transient in charger current to regulate battery voltage, especially during telemetry PING. This current transient is present on the PLC line and could lead to potential PLC communication failure. It is recommended to use CC track mode during this charging phase.

## Maintain Charge State

The charger enters the Maintain Charge state from the CC Track state or CV state when the charge current drops below IChgDone. In the Maintain Charge state, the charger regulates the battery voltage at BatReg and starts the Maintain Charge timer. The charge current is equal to or lower than  IChgDone. The charge current keeps decreasing in the Maintain Charge state while the battery voltage is regulated at BatReg.

The following events cause the charger state machine to exit the Maintain Charge state:

- If the charger auto-stop is enabled (ChgAutoStop = 1) and the maintain charge timer MtChgTmr expires, the charger enters Maintain Charge Timer Done state and stops charging.
- If the charge current rises above IChgDone, the charger goes back to the CV state, and reset the step charge timer CC1FChgTmr.
- JEITA disable charging. The Charger suspends charging due to temperature and enters the Maintain Charge Timer Done state. See the JEITA compliance section for a detailed description.

If the charger auto-stop is disabled (ChgAutoStop = 0), the charger ignores the maintain charger timer and stays in the Maintain Charge state even if the charger current drops below IChgDone. When the charger transitions in and out of the Maintain Charge state, the charger safety timer does not reset. Only the local timer resets. If the charger enters the Maintain Charge state, the maintain charge timer MtChgTmr resets and starts counting. If the charger exits the Maintain Charge state and enters the CV state, the step charge timer CC1IFChgTmr resets.

## Maintain Charge Timer Done State

Charger enters the Maintain Charge Timer Done state from the Maintain Charge state after the Maintain Charge timer MtChgTmr is expired and charger auto-stop enabled ChgAutoStop = 1 or when JEITA suspends charging. In the Maintain Charge Timer Done state, the charger stops charging. If the charger is enabled ChgEn = 1 and the charger auto-restart is enabled ChgAutoReSta = 1, the charger exits the Maintain Charge Timer Done state and resumes charging once the battery voltage falls below recharge threshold BatReg - BatReChg. If the charger auto-restart is enabled ChgAutoReSta = 1 and the system is placed in PLC IDLE mode after the earbud battery is fully charged, the MAX20357A automatically exits PLC IDLE mode and resumes charging once the battery voltage falls below recharge threshold BatReg - BatReChg.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## Charger Timers

The battery charger provides a global charger safety timer ChgTmr and three local timers, a precharge timer PChgTmr, a step-charge timer CC1FChgTmr, and a maintain charge timer MtChgTmr to ensure safe charging. These timers prevent the battery from being overcharged.

The safety timer ChgTmr runs through all charging states that have non-zero charger current, including Precharge , CC1 , CC2 , CC Track , CV ,  and Maintain Charge .  When the charger safety timer ChgTmr expires, the charger enters the Charger Fault - Safety Timer ChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1001) and stops charging.

Precharge  timer  PChgTmr  runs  in  Precharge  state.  Precharge  timer  PChgTmr  resets  when  the  charger  enters  the Precharge state, except from JEITA suspend charging state (ChgStat = 0b1111). When the precharge timer PChgTmr expires, the charger enters the Charger Fault -PChgTmr Expired state (ChgStat = 0b1000) and stops charging.

The charger can exit the safety timer fault or precharge timer fault state by toggling ChgEn, soft reset, or removing and re-connection of PLC. When the charger enters the Charger Off state (ChgStat = 0b000) or Charger IDLE state (ChgStat = 0b0001) by the above behavior, the charger can exit from the charger fault state.

Step-charge timer CC1FChgTmr runs in CC1 state. The step-charge timeout does not lead to any fault state. When it expires, the charger is forced to enter the CC2 state. Step-charge timer CC1IFChgTmr resets when the charger enters the CC1 state from the Precharge state, or when the charger goes from the Maintain Charge state to the CV state. Due to battery internal resistance and cable/PCB trace resistance, it is common that battery voltage detected from the BAT pin drops when the charger goes from CC1 to CC2 state. It is caused by reduced charge current from CC1IFChg to CC2IFChg. If the charger enters CC2 due to step-charge timeout, even if the battery voltage detected from the BAT pin drops below step-charge threshold ChgStepRise - ChgStepHys, it stays in the CC2 state. StepChgDone is reset to 0 when the charger enters the charger boot sequence.

In the CC1 state, CC2 state, and CC Track state, both the local step-charge timer CC1FChgTmr (in CC1 state) and the global charger safety timer ChgTmr are slowed by 50% and the fast-charge current is reduced to be between 20% and 50% of CC1/CC2 charge current. These two timers are paused when the fast-charge current is reduced to be below 20% of  the  CC1/CC2  charge  current.  Note  the  slowdown  of  the  above  timers  applies  to  any  type  of  fast-charge  current reduction, see the Reduced Charge Current section.

## Reduced Charge Current

Charger current can be less than the programmed value (IPChg, CC1IFChg, CC2IFChg) for any of the following reasons:

- JEITA controlled fast-charging current (CC1IFChg, CC2IFChg). When thermistor monitoring is enabled and the fastcharging current in cool, room, or warm temperature zone is programmed to be lower than 1xCC1IFChg/CC2IFChg.
- The SYS LDO is enabled, and the PLC current limit is reached. The charge current is reduced in favor of the system load. System load always gets priority over the charger current.
- The SYS LDO is disabled, but the PLC current limit is lower than the programmed charger current. The charger current is regulated at the PLC current limit.
- The charger is under thermal regulation, see the Thermal Regulation section.

If the charger drop-out voltage drops down to the programmable threshold PLC\_DROP, the charger current is actively reduced to maintain the charger input and output voltage difference V PLC -V BAT  at PLC\_DROP. As shown in Figure 16 , when the charger current is being actively reduced, some charger state transitions are unavailable.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## JEITA Compliance

To  enhance  safety  when  charging  Li+  batteries,  the  MAX20357A  features  JEITA-compliant  temperature  monitoring. Temperature measurement using an external NTC thermistor places the battery into one of five temperature zones: cold, cool, room, warm, and hot. When JEITA-compliant thermistor monitoring is enabled, charging is always inhibited in cold and hot regions or if the thermistor cannot be detected. Charging behavior is configurable in warm, room, and cool regions using the I 2 C-controlled ThmEn parameter, as demonstrated in Figure 17 to Figure 20 . Maintain charge state (ChgStat = 0b0110) only runs in the room temperature zone when thermistor monitoring is enabled.

CC1 state has its dedicated I 2 C-programmed temperature thresholds, which can be configured using the ChgT1ThrCC1ChgT4ThrCC1. I 2 C-programmed temperature thresholds of precharge state, CC2 state, CC Track state, and CV state can be configured using ChgT1ThrDef-ChgT4ThrDef. JEITA's current control strategy in cool, room, and warm zones is programmable with the ThmCfg0-ThmCfg2 as shown in Figure 17 to Figure 20 .

Note that JEITA measurements are continuous only if PLC is present. If PLC is not present and the ThmEn setting is higher than 0x3, JEITA measurement is performed based on ModelGauge m5 EZ measurements at a maximum rate set by jta\_eoc\_sel.

## Thermal Regulation

In addition to the PLC input current limit, the MAX20357A features thermal charge current regulation. This circuit acts to reduce charge current when the die temperature reaches a programmed thermal limit T SHDN\_PLC -3°C, preventing further temperature increase. T SHDN\_PLC  is I 2 C programmable through ChgThrmLim. If the SYS LDO is enabled, the thermal regulation loop first reduces the charge current in favor of the SYS load. As the charge current is reduced to zero, the charger is off. If the die temperature continues to increase, the thermal regulation loop reduces the SYS LDO current, and the system load is supplemented by the battery. ChgThrmReg and its corresponding interrupt indicate if the charger is under thermal regulation. If the current is reduced to 0 but the temperature still hits T SHDN\_PLC , the MAX20357A enters thermal shutdown mode, waiting for the temperature to drop below the threshold. The MAX20357A resumes operation once the temperature drops below the thermal threshold. Note that all the registers reset to default when the MAX2037A goes out from thermal shutdown. This feature is designed to provide second overheating protection for earbuds in addition to JEITA's current operation.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## SYS LDO and Power Path

Figure 21. Input Limiter, Power Path, and Charger Block Diagram

<!-- image -->

## SYS LDO Default Mode

After the power-on reset, SYS LDO delivers power to the SYS output from the PLC line. When PLC is present, the SYS LDO output voltage reference is set based on the following rule:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

If V BAT  is lower than the minimum system voltage SysMin, SYS LDO regulates SYS voltage at SysMin to maintain a constant supply voltage to prevent the earbud SoC from UVLO. If V BAT  is higher than the minimum system voltage SysMin, the SYS LDO works in parallel with the charger by default and regulates V SYS  = V BAT .

It should be noted that SYS LDO also decouples the SYS capacitance from the PLC line. On the PLC pin, no capacitance should be attached to allow full data rate communication.

## SYS LDO Bypass Mode

As illustrated in the previous sections, the PLC line is sensitive to load change, which may cause abrupt voltage change and corrupt the PING packet. The MAX20357A features bypass SYS LDO and support any transient load directly from the battery. When PLC is present, set SysMinForce = 1 to force the SYS LDO to regulate V SYS  = SysMin. The SYS pin voltage is regulated based on the following rule:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

When the battery voltage is sufficiently higher than the minimum system voltage SysMin (V BAT -PP\_drp &gt; SysMin), the power path FET is turned on and battery to SYS voltage drop is regulated to the programmable value PP\_drp. The SYS LDO attempts to regulate V SYS  = SysMin. However, due to the power path, V SYS  is clamped at PP\_drp lower than the battery voltage, which is higher than SysMin. SYS LDO eventually turns off. All system load is supplied by the battery. The PLC line current is regulated by the charger's constant current control loop. The PLC line is protected from any significant fluctuation caused by system load transient. To achieve reliable PLC communication and maintain a clean PLC channel, it is highly recommended to set SysMinForce = 1 when the battery voltage is sufficiently higher than SysMin. When the battery voltage is not sufficiently higher than SysMin (V BAT -PP\_drp &lt; SysMin), the power path is open and the SYS LDO regulates V SYS  = SysMin. System load is supplied from the PLC line. The system designer needs to avoid applying any transient load during PLC PING since it may cause PLC communication failure and disconnection of PLC.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## Battery Only Mode

When the PLC is not present and the battery is alive, the power path FET is fully ON, and the system load is supplied by the battery.

## Reverse Protection

The SYS LDO and the charger feature reverse protection preventing reverse conduction when V SYS  or V BAT  is higher than the PLC charging voltage. When the voltage difference between the two earbud batteries is large, and the minimum algorithm is elected in the MAX20355A, the PLC charging voltage is adjusted to track the lower earbud battery of the two. In the earbud with the higher battery voltage, V SYS  and V BAT  are higher than the PLC charging voltage. In this condition, the SYS LDO and the charger are in reverse protection and the current is not back-driving the PLC line. During reverse protection, the MAX20355A and MAX20357A are still exchanging battery voltage and SOC information through telemetry PINGs. The reverse protection feature allows the MAX20355A to track either battery without concern of back-driving the PLC line.

## Mono-Slave and Dual-Slave Mode

The MAX20357A supports dual-slave mode to connect to PLC1 or PLC2 on the MAX20355A, or mono-slave mode if PLC1 and PLC2 on MAX20355A are shorted. The PLC input current upper limit is programmable through PLCCurr with a maximum value of 200mA or 400mA depending on the state of the Ichg\_x2 bit. The function Ichg\_x2 bit is summarized as follows:

1.  Ichg\_x2 = 0. In dual-slave mode, the PLC input current limit is set to operate in the range up to 200mA, SYS LDO output current and the charger current are both limited to PLCCurr, up to 200mA.
2.  Ichg\_x2 = 1. When PLC1 and PLC2 of the MAX20355A are shorted in mono-slave mode, Ichg\_x2 can be set to 1 to double the PLC input current PLCCurr to 400mA. SYS LDO output current is limited by PLCCurr, up to 200mA. Charge current is limited by 2xPLCCurr, up to 400mA.

By setting Ichg\_x2 to 1, the MAX20357A  doubles the PLC current limit PLCCurr and charger current CC1IFChg/CC2IFChg. The MAX20357A achieves up to 400mA charge current in mono-slave mode. Note that since the MAX20355A needs to short PLC1 and PLC2, it may cause a current imbalance between the two channels (Refer to the MAX20355A data sheet for detailed description). The PLC solution does not support toggling between mono-slave and dual-slave mode during operation. Once the system is configured to mono-slave mode, PLC current sink PLCSnkSel and PLC voltage threshold PLCThrSel on both MAX20355A and MAX20357A are doubled automatically to compensate for higher buck-boost output voltage ripple created by PLC1 and PLC2 tied together.

## Battery Presence Detection

When pack protectors open due to a discharge-related fault, the pack protector turns off the discharge FET, placing a reverse-biased body diode in the discharge path and preventing further discharge. In this state, the system designer can decide that the battery has been damaged and that they would like to prevent a full charge cycle in the future. Even if the system designer does decide that the battery can be recovered, they can have concerns that the diode drop of the pack protector can cause the charger to believe that the battery is above the precharge voltage threshold, which would mean that the fast-charge current is applied.

In this scenario, it is useful for the system to understand before starting a full charge cycle whether a pack is present on the BAT node (with an open protector) or if the battery has simply been removed. The MAX20357A contains all of the necessary circuitry to allow the system designer to implement such a check.

One example of a simple algorithm to check for such a condition is to run the below check every time before starting a battery charging cycle:

1. Before enabling the charger, set BattPullDown = 1 to enable the pulldown resistor on the BAT node and monitor the BatUVLOB status bit. This pulldown resistor discharges the capacitance on the BAT node in case the battery is not present or the pack protector is open. After some time, check the BatUVLOB status and disable the BAT pulldown resistor.  If  BatUVLOB  =  1  (V BAT   &gt;  BAT\_UVLO),  then  the  battery  is  present,  and  charging  can  be  enabled.  If BatUVLOB = 0, the BAT voltage is below the UVLO threshold. The following are two possibilities for BatUVLOB = 0:
- a. The battery is not present.
- b. The pack protector is open.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

2. To distinguish between the above two possibilities, set FrcPChg = 1 and ChgEn = 1 to enable charging and force the charger to operate in the Precharge state (ChgStat = 0b0010) and monitor the ChgVoltMode status bit. Such charging current and duration can close the pack protector if there is a battery present. After some time, if ChgVoltMode = 0 (V BAT  &lt; V BAT\_REG ), this indicates that the pack protector is closed, and the battery is sinking the precharge current IPChg. Set FrcPChg = 0 to disable force precharge and the charger enters the normal charging states determined by the battery voltage. If ChgVoltMode = 1 (V BAT ≥ V BAT\_REG ), set FrcPChg = 0 and ChgEn = 0 to disable charging. The following are two possibilities for ChgVoltMode = 1:
- a. The battery is not present. The precharge current IPChg quickly charges the capacitance at the CHG\_OUT node.
- b. The battery is present and the forced precharge current IPChg closes the pack protector. However, the battery voltage is higher than the termination voltage BatReg. Repeat step 1, if BatUVLOB = 1, this indicates the battery is present as the pack protector has closed and V BAT  &gt; BAT\_UVLO.

Note that if the ChgGMD = 1 (V PLC  - V BAT  &lt; PLC\_DROP), the battery presence detection mentioned above is not reliable as the limiter is not allowing the charger to force any current into the BAT node.

If  the THM pin is connected to the thermistor inside the battery pack, battery insertion/removal detection can also be reported by the integrated fuel gauge. If the THM pin is connected to the thermistor on the PCB, use the procedure mentioned above to detect battery presence.

## PLC Operation Modes

The MAX20357A features multiple modes of operation that are designed to minimize power consumption in the end user's application. The transitions between modes and general operation are shown in Figure 22 .  The behavior of the main functional blocks in SEAL mode, OFF mode, and SYSUVLO mode are summarized in Table 5 .

| Fuel Gauge     | OFF                       | OFF                       | ON                        | ON                              |
| PLC            | Passive impedance network | Passive impedance network | Passive impedance network | Passive network not connected   |

## SYSULVO Mode

The MAX20357A enters SYSUVLO mode after first power up from the BAT pin, or when the SYS pin voltage drops below the SYSUVLO threshold. The device exits SYSUVLO mode when the device detects a PLC voltage higher than 3V. OTP registers are reloaded after the device exits SYSUVLO mode.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

## OFF Mode

The MAX20357A enters OFF mode by issuing an OFF mode request through I 2 C (write 1 to off\_cmd\_inp), or by driving the EN low if the EN pin function is enabled (enb\_otp\_ena = 1, default is 0) with no PLC pulse. In the OFF mode, SYS power is cut off, the fuel gauge remains on, I 2 C access is retained, and all other blocks are placed into a low-power mode. The device exits OFF mode when the device detects a PLC voltage higher than 3V (default) or the EN bit is driven high (enb\_otp\_ena = 1). OFF mode offers a low-power mode for the charge-done use case when the battery is fully charged but the slave device remains in the charging case.

## PLC Detection Mode

The standalone master or slave operates in PLC detection mode when the device is active. The PLC and moisture detection block diagrams are shown in Figure 23 . In PLC detection mode, the master sends a PLC detection pulse through an internal pullup/pulldown resistor every 380ms. The slave sends a PLC detection pulse through a similar pullup/pulldown resistor every 240ms. Master PLC is active, fuel gauge is on, and other blocks are in low-power mode; slave PLC is active, fuel gauge is on, power path FET is fully on, and other blocks are in a low-power state. Both the master and slave contain a passive impedance clamp network, which allows the PLC device to detect its counterpart device even when the counterpart device has a dead battery. See the following sections for a detailed description of the master and slave connection detection scheme under various circumstances. PLC detection pulse or moisture measurement launched by the counterpart device is shown in a dashed line.

Figure 23. PLC and Moisture Detection Block Diagrams

<!-- image -->

## Active Master and Active Slave

As shown in Figure 24 ,  both the master and slave are in the PLC detection mode before the slave is attached. After receiving the slave's PLC detection pulse on the PLC line, the master sends a signature pulse (3x pulse) and automatically launches a moisture measurement. If there is no moisture, the master connects the PLC line to a buck-boost output and holds the PLC voltage to the default charge voltage (default 3.5V) for 100ms. Then the master increases the PLC voltage to communication voltage (default 5.5V) and PING the slave. If the master received ACK from the slave, both devices enter PLC Link mode. After the master and slave build connection, the ch1/2\_con\_sts status bit of MAX20355A and chn\_con\_sts status bit of MAX20357A are set, and the corresponding interrupt bits are asserted accordingly.

If moisture is found, the master does not turn on the buck-boost to supply the PLC1/2 pin, and no power is applied to the slave. Moisture-related status and interrupt registers in MAX20355A are set indicating moisture detected. If the slave does not detect power applied through the PLC line within 200ms, it launches moisture measurement automatically. If moisture is not cleared, the slave stays in moisture detection mode and launches automatic moisture detection every 16s. Once the slave detects moisture is cleared, it proceeds to PLC detection mode to connect with the master. See the Moisture Detection section for a detailed description of the moisture feature.

## Power Line Communication with ModelGauge Fuel Gauge and Charger

Figure 24. Active Master and Active Slave Connection

## Active Master and Dead Slave

Both master and slave offer passive impedance clamp networks for dead slave battery detection and moisture detection. As shown in Figure 25 , before connection, the master is in PLC detection mode and the slave is connected to a dead battery. After the slave is attached to the master, the amplitude of the master's PLC detection pulse is clamped to 1.8V by the slave's internal passive impedance clamp network. The master perceives its PLC detection pulse with reduced amplitude as 'something connected'. After a 3x consecutive 'something connected' pulse, the master launches moisture measurement automatically. If there is no moisture, the master connects the PLC line to the buck-boost output and holds the PLC voltage to the default charge voltage (default 3.5V) for 100ms. Then the master increases the PLC voltage to communication voltage (default 5.5V) and PING the slave. If the master received ACK from the slave, both devices enter the  PLC  Link  mode.  After  the  master  and  slave  build  connection,  the  ch1/2\_con\_sts  status  bit  of  MAX20355A  and chn\_con\_sts status bit of MAX20357A are set, and the corresponding interrupt bits are asserted accordingly.

Note that not only does the passive impedance clamp on the slave create a 'something connected' pulse on the master, but moisture between the PLC line and the ground can also create similar behavior. The master is not able to determine if it is a real slave or moisture impedance before launching moisture detection. If moisture is found, the master does not connect the buck-boost output to the PLC1/2 pin, and power is not applied to the slave. Moisture-related status and interrupt registers in MAX20355A are set indicating moisture detected. The MAX20357A remains in SYSUVLO mode.

## Dead Master and Active Slave

As shown in Figure 26 before connection, the slave is in PLC Detection mode and the master is connected to a dead battery. After the slave is attached to the master, the slave's PLC detection pulse is clamped to 1.8V by the master's passive impedance clamp network. The slave p erceives its PLC detection pulse with reduced amplitude as 'something connected'.  After  a  3x  consecutive  'something  connected'  pulse,  a  dead\_found  interrupt  is  asserted  in  MAX20357A signaling dead master is detected, MAX20357A automatically launches a moisture measurement and continues sending a PLC detection pulse. After the master wakes up, the master and slave follow the procedure described in the Active Master and Active Slave section to build a connection.
